# Vedic Wisdom Tales

## Introduction

By the causeless mercy and inspiration of Paramaradhyatama Sri Guru-padapadma Nitya-lila-pravista От Visnupada Astottara-sata Sri Srimad Bhaktivedanta Narayana Gosvami Maharaja, we are overjoyed to present the first English translation of this anthology of instructive episodes entitled Vedic Wisdom Tales in honour of the 150th anniversary of the divine appearance of Prabhupada Srila Bhaktisiddhanta Sarasvati Thakura. The present edition is based on the original Bengali version published by the Gaudiya Mission (Bagbazar, Kolkata 1940) under the title Upakhyane Upadesa Volume Two and the Hindi edition published by the Sri Gaura Vraja Madhuri Samiti (Dhruva Ghata, Mathura 2014).

Upakhyane Upadesa Volume One is a compilation of Srila Prabhupada’s valuable instructions based on worldly and often fictitious anecdotes (laukika-upakhyana) and logic (laukika-nyaya) as found in traditional literature on ethics (niti-sastra) such as the Panca Tantra. Srila Prabhupada skilfully reinterprets the well-known worldly anecdotes to reveal practical applications for success in spiritual life. In contrast, Upakhyane Upadesa Volume Two presents the teachings often told by Srila Prabhupada based on factual incidents (vastava-upakhyana) from ancient Vedic scriptures such as the Upanisads, the Mahabharata, Srimad-Bhagavatam, and Sri Visnu Purana, and also from later texts such as Sri Prapannamrta, Sri Caitanya Bhagavata and Sri Narottama Vilasa.

The meaning of the term ‘upakhyana’ is not limited to the sense of‘anecdote’ or ‘imaginary episode’ from a novel. Rather, the ‘puravrtta, incidents presented in the Vedic histories (puranas), are also called upakhyana. In this regard, Srila Jiva GosvamI Prabhu has cited a verse from the Vayu Purana in his Tattva Sandarbha (Anuccheda 14):

*akhyanais capy upakhyanair gathabhir dvija-sattamah*  
*purana-samhitas cakrepuranartha-visaradah*

*Vayu Purana 1.60.21*

**О    best of the twice-born! Srila Vedavyasa is most proficient in the meaning of the Puranas. Thereafter, he compiled the Puranas and the Mahabharata (Samhita) by collating various types of narrative categorized as akhyanas, upakhyanas and gathas.**

Defining the terms akhyana, upakhyana and gatha, Sridhara Svami has commented:

*svayam drstartha-kathanam prahur*  
*akhyanakam budhah srutasyarthasya*  
*kathanam upakhyanam pracaksate*  
*gathas tu pitr-prthivy-adi-gitayah*

*Sridhara Svami’s commentary on*  
*Sri Visnu Purana 3.6.1*

**Learned sages define akhyana as the description of an incident personally witnessed by the speaker, whereas an upakhyana is the description of an event received by hearing. A gatha is a song about forefathers and earthly beings.**

Gaudiya Vedantacarya Srila Baladeva Vidyabhusana Prabhu has explained the aforementioned verse (Vayu Purana 1.60.21) thus:

*akhyanaih - paficalaksaneh puranani;*  
*upakhyanaih - puravrtte: gathabhih - chandovisesaisca*

Here, 'akhyana’ means 'purana’, a scriptural history with five characteristics \[(l) sarga - creation, (2) pratisarga - dissolution, (3) vamsa - genealogies of kings and sages, (4) manvantara - the reigns of Manus, (5) vamsanucarita - histories of various avataras, sages and kings\]; ‘upakhyana’ means ‘puravrtta', a historical incident; and ‘gatha’ refers to a particular type of song about forefathers, etc.

Thirty-four scriptural 'upakhyanas‘ and the instructions (upadesa) or wise counsel received from them have been collected in this precious book. Therefore, we have rendered the original title Upakhyane Upadesa in simple English as Vedic Wisdom Tales. The general masses of people today are fond of fiction. Very few are seen to be interested in listening to complex ontological principles. Therefore, Srila Bhaktisiddhanta Sarasvati Prabhupada, who feels distress upon seeing the distress of others (paraduhkha duhkhi) and who has taken a vow to administer to the welfare of others (paropakara vratadhari), has tried to convey the profound teachings of the Vedas, Srimad Bhagavad-gita, Srimad-Bhagavatam and the Puranas in the simple and accessible form of short stories.

In this volume, you will find the immaculate ideals of pure devotional life, the stellar character of the divine masters (atimartya caritra), and the essential philosophical conclusions of the Vedas. Although many of these historical episodes will be familiar to persons acquainted with Indian culture, this book departs entirely from the cliched interpretations and worldly conceptions arising from the conditioned thoughts (manodharma) of non-liberated intellectuals. The substantive conclusions of these episodes are received through the inner revelation of divine sound (irauta-siddhanta) spoken by the liberated associates of Sri Caitanya Mahaprabhu in the line of Sri Rupa Gosvami such as От Visnupada Astottara-sata Sri Srimad Bhaktisiddhanta Sarasvatl GosvamI Prabhupada. Like the ripened fruit of the wish-fulfilling tree of the Vedas emanating from the mouth of Srila Sukadeva GosvamI, this nectarean advice infuses the consciousness with spiritual energy (sakti-sancarakari) and destroys all psychological obstacles (anartha-vinasakari). Spiritual seekers who desire their own most auspicious well-being will be able to glean many ingredient components (upadana) for the formation of a life of pure devotion from these divinely inspired statements (srauta-vakya).

Finally, with immense gratitude, we acknowledge the service of Sriman Manikanta dasa and Sriman Gauraraja dasa brahmacarl for preparing the manuscript; Sriman Sikhi Mahiti dasa brahmacarl and Sriman Ananta Nitai dasa for the illustrations and cover design; Srimati Indira-devi dasi for layout; and Srimati Anupama dasi, Srimati Jahnava Thakurani dasi, Srimati Vrndavana-vilasini dasi and Srimati Vrnda dasi for their dedicated service in editing and proofreading. We are also grateful to the Mankani family for their generous support. May Sri Gaurasundara shower His mercy upon them. If the respected readers discover any error in the text, we beg your forgiveness. Kindly inform us so that we may improve the next edition.

Sri-hari-guru-vaisnava-krpa-lesa-prarthi, dina hina, Sri Prema Prayojana dasa 150th Appearance Day of Prabhupada Srila Bhaktisiddhanta Sarasvati Thakura Thursday, February 29th 2024

## Chapter 1"I am Geat" and "I am Good"

Once there was a fierce battle between the legions of the demigods and the demons. The demigods were extremely enthusiastic to defeat the demons. They succeeded in defeating the demons only by the influence of the power of Bhagavan (God). However, the demigods forgot about Bhagavan’s mercy and accredited the victory to their own physical strength and military expertise. Thus, the demigods became swollen with pride and basked in the respect and praise of the public. To dispel this foolishness and hypocrisy, Bhagavan changed His form and became visible at a short distance from the demigods. They were unable to recognize this disguised form of the Supreme Lord, so they said to Agni (the demigod of fire), “Who is this worshipful person who has appeared before us? Go and investigate.”

As soon as Agni approached Him, that great personality asked, “Who are you?”

Agni replied, “I am Agni - I am the famous Jata Vedah!”

Bhagavan inquired, “What power do you have?”

Agni boldly asserted, “I can burn the whole earth to ashes in an instant.”

Then Bhagavan placed a dry straw in front of Agni and requested, “Demonstrate your power by burning this piece of straw.” Agni approached the piece of straw but, even after exerting his whole might, the straw remained intact.

Then Agni returned to the demigods and informed them, “I could not recognize that exalted person.”

When Agni failed, the demigods decided to send Vayu (the wind demigod) to discover the identity of that maha-purusa (great person). The demigod Vayu set off and approached Bhagavan.

Bhagavan asked, “Who are you?”

Vayu replied, “I am Matarisva (the wind-god).”

Bhagavan asked, “What power do you have?”

Vayu said, “I can blow away everything on the face of the earth.”

Then Bhagavan placed a piece of straw in front of Vayu and asked him to move it. Even after applying all his power, Vayu could not move that insignificant piece of straw even a tiny bit. Crestfallen, Vayu returned to the demigods and reported, “I could not recognize that great being.”

Finally, the demigods appointed Devaraja Indra, the king of the demigods, to solve the mystery. As soon as Indra approached that great person, He disappeared from view. At that moment, Indra saw the exquisitely beautiful goddess Uma Devi, the consort of Siva, appear in the sky. Approaching Uma Devi, Indra asked, “Who is this maha-purusa?”

Uma Devi informed him, “This is Brahma, the Absolute Truth. You demigods became glorious only by His victory. Your power is manifest only by His power. When He withdraws His power, you do not have any power of your own. The original owner of your power (ksamata), expertise (daksata), heroism (virata), and prowess (paurusa) is only the Supreme Brahma. He is the musician. You are just His instrument. When you think that you are doing everything with your own strength, then at once Brahma takes away all your power.”

Those who disregard the power of Sri Guru and Bhagavan desire to use the eternally acquired prosperity, worship and prestige (labha, puja and pratistha) of Sri Guru and Bhagavan for their own enjoyment. Then the Lord, the spiritual master and the saints take away their proficiency. When the soul employs his abilities and energy sincerely in the service of God, exclusively for the pleasure of the Lord, only then does he become krpa-prarthi, truly desirous of the grace of Hari, Sri Guru and vaisnavas (saints). Conversely, when a soul uses his God-given potential to nourish his arrogance (dambhikata) or when he becomes hostile (vidvesa) towards the spiritual master and pure devotees, then he meets with complete ruination. The only source of all power is the Supreme Lord. All wealth, women, profit, worship, and prestige are obtained only from Him.

*pratisthasa taru -jadamaya mаru*  
*na pelo ravana jujhiya raghava*  
*vaisnava pratistha, ta’te’kara' nistha*  
*taha na bhajile laghibe raurava*

*Vaisnava Ke, verse 4*  
*by Srila Bhaktisiddhanta Sarasvati Thakura*

**The demon Ravana (lust-incarnate) fought with Lord Ramacandra (love-incarnate) in order to gain the tree of worldly reputation-but that oasis turned out to be but a mirage cast in the desert wasteland of the Lord’s illusory material potency. Please cultivate fixed determination to attain only that steady and solid platform upon which a vaisnava ever stands. If you neglect to worship the Lord from this position, then you will ultimately attain a hellish existence.**

Ravana fought Rama in order to achieve the highest prestige (pratistha), but that prestige was merely an illusion. His hope for prestige had grown as mighty as a great tree within the forest of desires within his heart, but the soul can find no cooling shade there, for the ‘forest’ is really just a mirage in the desert of material illusion. Do not be like Ravana, who wanted to enjoy the position of Rama. Accept the position of the servant of Visnu. Take your stand upon the foundation of pure devotional service, giving up all your hopes for false prestige, and thus become a genuine vaisnava (saint). If you do not worship the position of the vaisnava, you will be doomed.

Due to pride in his fame, Ravana wanted to take the seat of Sri Ramacandra Himself. He was so egotistical that he thought he could defeat Bhagavan in battle. He achieved nothing from this. Rather, he was destroyed. When a living being neglects the power of the Lord and takes pride in his own ability, he receives a similar reward. The sakti given by Bhagavan can be used in a proper way only by devoting all prosperity, worship and prestige to the Lord, the spiritual master and the saints. Considering oneself as the servant of the servant (dasanudasa) of Krsna and keeping oneself employed in the service of guru, vaisnavas and Bhagavan, one must abandon the arrogant attitude characterized by the notion “I am great.” That is, “I am the doer. I am an independent agent. I am the enjoyer. I can do everything by my own strength.” Instead, one should think, “I am an insignificant jiva (soul). I am an eternal servant of Sri Hari, guru and vaisnavas. My wealth is due to the Lord’s mercy. He is the yantri (operator), I am just a yantra (instrument).” Jagad-guru Srila Prabhupada used to recite this story from the Kena Upanisad to advise us to nurture a guileless heart. The false notion of metaphysical independence (svatantrata) leads to the arrogance (dambhikata) that “I am great.” and “I am good.” One should endeavour for self-reformation (atma-samsodhana) and be purified of this mentality by remaining under the constant discipline (sasanadhina) of one’s spiritual masters (srj guru-varga) with a sincere desire to attain divine mercy (krpa).

## Chapter 2 Brahma, Indra and Virocana

Once upon a time, Lord Brahma explained, “The atma is beyond sin, virtue, old age, grief, hunger, thirst, resolve and uncertainty. Those who investigate this soul in accordance with the teachings of the scriptures and acaryas can realize it. Only such persons can become invested with all glories. Then, the opulence of unlimited universes runs behind them to render service.”

When rumours about Lord Brahma’s words reached the ears of both the devatas (demigods) and the asuras (demons), they began to discuss amongst themselves, “As Lord Brahma has described, all opulence, which is attained along with realization of the atma, is waiting for the chance to serve us. What is the harm in investigating the atma?" After deliberating in this way, Indra, on behalf of the demigods, and Virocana, on behalf of the asuras, set off on the journey to approach Lord Brahma. In the absence of friendship between them, and being unable to bear the prospect of the other attaining this knowledge, they grew envious of each other. Finally, carrying firewood for sacrifice, they appeared before Lord Brahma with folded hands. They took up residence in the asrama of their guru, Lord Brahma, and observed the vow of celibate student life (brahmacarya-vrata) for thirty-two years.

After this, one day Brahma asked them, “For what purpose have you come here?”

They replied, “Some time ago, you described that all opulence becomes subordinate to that person who can realize the atma in the lotus of his heart. After hearing about your important teaching, we have come to you to search out that immortal soul.”

Lord Brahma said, “After abandoning all material desires (visaya-vasana), the Purusa whom the great yogis observe in their eyes is the atma. Only this Parabrahma, the Absolute Truth, is the basis of fearlessness (abhaya) and immortality (amrta), devoid of sorrow (asoka)."

Neither Indra nor Virocana were able to comprehend the purport of this teaching of Lord Brahma. They inquired, “O fortunate one! Among the reflections we see in reflective media such as water or a mirror, which of these is atma?"

Lord Brahma replied, “That atma is present in all substances. After beholding your respective souls in this vessel of water, if there is anything you do not understand, you can ask me.” Both Indra and Virocana began to stare into that water-filled vessel with one-pointed concentration, but they did not ask anything. Seeing this, Lord Brahma asked them, “What do you see?”

Then both of them replied, “Lord! We are looking at the atma and its reflection from the pores of the skin to the fingernails.”

Then Lord Brahma instructed them, “Now cut your hair and fingernails. Then decorate yourselves with celestial ornaments and look at yourselves again in the water pot.” After following his instructions, Lord Brahma again asked them, “Now what do you see?”

They replied, “We see only the reflection with cut hair and nails, adorned with beautiful ornaments.”

Then, Brahma understood that they were not yet qualified to realize atma-tattva. Perhaps later they would be able to take this teaching to heart. Thinking in this way, Lord Brahma said, “This is atma. This is the immortal and fearless absolute (brahma)."

Then Indra and Virocana began the return journey to their homes. Seeing them set out, Lord Brahma said, “Both of them are leaving without having realized the atma. Whoever accepts the theory regarding atma-tattva from these two, whether he is a demigod or a demon, will deviate from the real path.”

Virocana, the king of the asuras, appeared before the asuras with a calm heart and taught them his misunderstanding of atma-tattva: “This body is the soul. It is this body that is worshipable in the world. It is only through the service of the body that one attains this world and the next world.” Due to the preaching of Virocana, the false doctrine of dehatma-vada (the body is the soul) is being propagated in this world today. People of demonic nature are initiated into such misconceptions. That is why such people think that if a person’s corpse is adorned with fragrance, garlands and splendid clothes, they will be happy in the next world.

On the other hand, while Indra was on his way to heaven, he began to reflect upon Lord Brahma’s words again and again. He thought, “There is a deep mystery in the advice that Lord Brahma has given. In fact, Lord Brahma has given such advice only to convey that the reflection is not an abiding, eternal substance. Therefore, to research the eternal substance, it would be appropriate to surrender fully and listen to his words again.”

Deliberating in this way and bearing wood for sacrifice in his hands, Indra again approached Brahma. Seeing Indra, Brahma asked, “O Indra! Just now you had gone away satisfied along with Virocana. So why have you returned?”

Indra replied, “O Master! A thought arose in my heart. After cutting the hair and nails, if this body is adorned with celestial ornaments, the reflection of the body visible in the water will appear in exactly the same way. Similarly, if someone is blind, has broken arms and legs, and water is flowing from his eyes and nose due to the attack of a severe disease, the reflection will appear accordingly. When this body is destroyed, the reflection will also be destroyed. Therefore, there is no benefit for me in knowing this reflection. This can never be the atma."

Brahma said, “O Indra! You have spoken correctly. I will repeat the instruction I gave you earlier regarding the atma. You could not understand its meaning at that time. So hear it again after serving in the residence of the spiritual master for another thirty-two years.”

Indra followed Lord Brahmas advice. Then Lord Brahma instructed Indra, “The one who roams around joyfully in the dream state is the atma. He is the immortal soul or brahma, the remover of all fears.”

After receiving this advice from Lord Brahma, Indra became elated and departed. Yet even before reaching the other demigods, he began to consider, “If a person is blind in the waking state, but experiences having the power of sight in his dream, then what truth is there in this reflection of seeing? What will be the benefit of considering the dream-self as ‘atma'? In dreams it may seem as if people are killing him; it may seem as if people are chasing him; it may seem as if there is something unpleasant. He may even seem to be weeping. I see nothing good in this.”

Pondering in this way, Indra again returned to Brahma, carrying wood for sacrifice in his hands, and expressed his deliberation on the subject. Brahma asked Indra to stay in the guru-grha for another thirty-two years and engage in sravana (hearing). Indra followed his advice. Then Brahma said, “The atma that is manifest in the state of susupti (deep sleep), that is the fearless, immortal soul or brahma.”

Being satisfied with this instruction of Lord Brahma, Indra set off towards heaven. But after some contemplation upon this, a doubt appeared in his mind. Carrying sacrificial wood, he again returned to Brahma. Revealing his doubt to his gurudeva, he said, “If this atma is illuminated by his own glory, then why does our sense of 'I' that is flowing continuously in the waking state and the dream state stop in the state of deep sleep? You are saying that the atma is unchanging in nature (sat-svarupa).” Then Lord Brahma asked Indra to stay with him for another five years.

Finally, Lord Brahma gave Indra this instruction: "The atma is actually dehi (embodied). The gross body of five elements and the dream body (svapnika-deha), which is called linga-sarira (subtle body), are merely the coverings of the atma.”

*evam evaisa samprasado 'smat sarirat samutthaya*  
*param jyotir upasampadya svena*  
*rupenabhinispadyate sa uttamah purusah sa tatra*  
*paryati jaksat kridati ramamanah*

*Chandogya Upanisad 8.12.3*

**After attaining liberation, this living being is completely liberated from the gross and subtle bodies. He becomes established in a transcendental form full of effulgence (he attains the real self and experiences it). He is the most exalted person. In that spiritual world, he remains immersed in relishing, playing and the bliss of loving union, etc.**

By completely surrendering himself, staying in the residence of the spiritual master (guru-grha), and listening to the words of Gurudeva, Indra was able to attain this truth and become fully accomplished and successful.

This episode from the Chandogya Upanisad proves that although two disciples approached a jagad-guru like Lord Brahma, because they had ulterior motives (anyabhilasa), they understood the instructions of the authentic spiritual teacher in different ways. Virocana, the king of the demons, could not comprehend the true meaning of his guru's words. Consequently, he propagated an opinion completely contrary to his guru while claiming to represent the opinion of the guru and the scriptures. The demons accepted his teachings and are still doing so today. On the other hand, Indra, the king of the demigods, tolerantly cultivated knowledge of atma through surrender (pranipata), humble inquiry (pariprasna) and service (seva). As long as Indra had not completely heard and realized his gurudeva’s words, he did not think, “Now I have understood everything.” He did not become ambitious to gain dominance over the demons. Only a disciple who surrenders himself to the guru and is devoted to service (seva parayana) can achieve atma-tattva. By outwardly pretending, like a professional actor in a theatre, to surrender, inquire and serve the authentic teacher, and by harbouring extraneous material desires in the heart, one is bound to become a propagator of a conception different from that of Gurudeva. Although the mango and neem trees on the banks of the Ganga consume the same water from the Ganga, the mango tree gives sweet fruits and the neem tree gives only bitter fruits. This is not the fault of the Ganga water. There is no miserliness (krpanata) in the dispensation of the Ganga. Rather, the trees give results according to their qualification (yogyata). Similarly, after approaching the same authentic guru, one person realizes the truth of krsna-tattva and another propagates a conception opposed to that of his gurudeva and tries to destroy the principles of pure devotion. Both Sri Isvara Purl and Ramacandra Purl approached Srila Madhavendra Puri, the root of the desire tree of divine love. Both of them were initiated into the sannyasa mantra by him. But by pretending to be blessed by the grace of such a sad-guru, Ramacandra Puri became deprived of the same, i.e. he became a propagator of impersonalism. Srila Isvara Puri received real mercy, i.e. he became completely surrendered to his sri guru-pada-padma.

*sei haite isvara-puri—’premera sagara’*  
*ramacandra-puri haila sarva-nindakara*

*Sri Caitanya-caritamrta, Antya-lila 8.31*

**Thus, Isvara Puri became like an ocean of ecstatic love for Krsna, whereas Ramacandra Puri became a dry speculator and a critic of everyone else.**

*mahad-anugraha-nigrahera ’saksi’ dui-jane*  
*ei dui-dvare sikhaila jaga-jane*

*Sri Caitanya-caritamrta, Antya-lila 8.32*

**Isvara Puri received the blessing of Madhavendra Puri, whereas Ramacandra Puri received a rebuke from him. Therefore, these two persons, Isvara Puri and Ramacandra Puri, are examples of the objects of a great personality’s benediction and punishment. Madhavendra Puri instructed the entire world by presenting these two examples.**

## Chapter 3 Naciketah

In ancient times, the famous King Auddalaki performed the ritual sacrifice called Visvajit with the hope of attaining heaven, during which he gave everything in charity. He had a son named ‘Naciketah’ Although he was just a child, he was very intelligent and committed to truthfulness. When his father began to make a vow to donate some barren cows, Naciketah considered, “One who donates in ritual charity cows such as these, which are unable to give milk or give birth to calves, will certainly go to a joyless world called Ananda.

Thinking in this way, Naciketah asked his father, “O Father! To whom will you give me as ritual compensation (daksina)?” The king did not answer his question. Again, Naciketah asked the same question to his father without getting any answer.

When Naciketah repeated the question once again, then King Auddalaki retorted in a fit of anger, “I will send you to Yama (demigod of death)!”

After hearing these words of his father, Naciketah sat alone in contemplation. He wondered, “What is the specific objective of Yama that my father wants to accomplish through me?”

Considering this, Naciketah said to his father, “All humans wither away like crops and die. Then they take birth again. Therefore, there is no difficulty for me to go to the abode of Yama.”

In obedience to his father’s words, Naciketah went to Yamapuri. When he arrived, Yama was not at home. Naciketah stayed in the abode of Yamalaya for three nights at that time. Later, when Yama returned, his wife said, “A hungry unexpected guest has come to our house. We should extend our hospitality.”

After properly honouring and worshipping Naciketah, Yama said to him, “You have become a guest in my house and you have been hungry for three nights. This is an offence on my part. Therefore, you may ask for one boon for each night.”

Then Naciketah requested, “O Yamaraja! The first boon I beg for is this: My father has become upset with me. Now he should be pleased with me. And when I go to him, may he recognize me and honour me with affection.”

Yama granted the boon by saying, “Tathastu (so be it).”

Then Naciketah said, “The people in heaven do not fear you. They are not subject to hunger, thirst or insufficiency. Everyone there is blissful. Please grant me the scientific knowledge of the fire sacrifice by which people go to heaven and attain immortality.”

Yamaraja said, “The fire to which you refer is the means to attain the eternal Visnuloka (abode of God) and the shelter of the entire world.” Considering Naciketah a worthy disciple, he said, “You can ask for one more boon in addition to the original three boons. The fire you wish to know about will become famous for being named after you. Now ask for the third boon.”

Naciketah said, “Some say there is a soul, some say there is not. I want to know your teachings and principles in this matter.”

Yama replied, “Even the demigods had doubts about this earlier. This topic is very subtle. Don’t request this from me. You should ask for some other boon - I can grant you all the boons such as sons, grandsons, many cows, animals, elephants, horses, a huge kingdom, heaven, and as many years of life as you want. You can ask according to your desire for everything that is very difficult to attain in the human body. You can ask for celestial women who are full of beauty and youth, adorned with fame and virtues, riding upon chariots and expertly playing on musical instruments. You can lead a very happy life with them. I can grant you this boon right now. Just do not ask questions related to death because it is exceedingly confidential.”

Yama exhibited various temptations to Naciketah. Naciketah said, “O Yamaraja! I don’t want any of the things you are tempting me with. All these things are subject to death. Nothing will remain. Whatever is in the world today will cease to exist tomorrow. Even if you grant a lifespan of a hundred years, a thousand years or hundreds of thousands of years, it is still bound to be destroyed. What is a lifespan of a hundred thousand years compared to eternity? The vitality of all the senses also becomes destroyed while engaged in the maintenance of worldly life. One’s strength also diminishes. You can keep the chariots, elephants, horses, beautiful women and the immense wealth of earth or heaven. I have no need for them. Money and wealth can never satisfy anyone. Especially when I have attained the audience of a great personality like you, then I have automatically obtained all opulence and a long life. There is no need for me to ask separately for this. I don’t want to ask for anything else. Just tell me about this ‘soul’ (atma). No intelligent person hopes to live for a long time because there is only sorrow in the material body. As one’s age increases, diseases attack the body. There is only disturbance in the material body, not peace. Although a fortunate soul may remain healthy, he cannot pass his life on this earth without disturbance. О Yamaraja! For the sake of my welfare, remove the doubt about the existence or non-existence of the soul and teach me atma-tattva. I will not ask for anything else besides this highly confidential boon related to the spiritual world.”

Seeing Naciketah so desirous to know atma-tattva, Yamaraja said, “You are rejecting preyah, that which gives pleasure at the present moment, and endeavouring to know sreyah, that which ultimately concludes in auspiciousness. Therefore, I praise you. The service of Bhagavan is sreyah (auspicious) and the desire for women, sons, wealth, etc. is preyah. These two are quite distinct. Those who follow the path of sreyah are freed from the bondage of material existence and those who desire preyah are deviated from the ultimate purpose of life (parama prayojana) and become bound in material conditions. Sreyah and preyah both lie before the living entity. An intelligent person understands both well, and carefully considers which one will lead to bondage and which one will lead to deliverance. Sober people accept only auspicious things. They follow the path of sreyah. Foolish persons follow the path of preyah by trying to attain worldly objects and desiring only to protect those things. Seeing that you do not have any kind of material desire, I am satisfied to know that you exclusively desire knowledge of the Supreme Truth (brahma). Just as a blind person can never guide another blind person to his destination, in the same way, those duplicitous and ignorant fools who consider themselves intelligent or learned, being entangled in preyah, sometimes go to heaven and sometimes to hell. They can never attain the truly desirable destination or show others the real path. The essential means of attaining the next world, that is, atma-tattva, is not revealed to such persons who are in the grip of infatuation. For all those who are attached to inert sense objects, there is no other world or real truth beyond this material world. Such a conception obliges them to taste the suffering of death again and again.

*sravanayapi bahubhir yo na labhyah*  
*srnvanto’pi bahavo yam na vidyuh*  
*ascaryo vakta kusalo’sya labdha*  
*ascaryo jnata kusalanusistah*

*Katha Upanisad 1.2.7*

**Very few people have the good fortune to hear about the atma. Among those who hear about the atma, most do not understand. This is because a self-realized teacher or sad-guru is extremely rare in this world. Even if one finds such a teacher by good fortune, qualified listeners or disciples are extremely rare.**

*naisa tarkena matir apaneya proktanyenaiva sujnanaya prestha*

*Katha Upanisad 1.2.9*

“O my dearest Naciketah, do not destroy with dry logic the firm determination (sudrdha-mati) that you have acquired to know the principle of Bhagavan. Devotion to Bhagavan is ruined by dry logic. I tested you in various ways to remove your curiosity about atma-tattva, but you remained patient and passed the test. A person without faith and knowledge of relationship (sambandha-jnana) can never attain realization of this eternal soul. Only those who hear atma-tattva from great souls and assimilate it into their life can attain that blissful Bhagavan and experience the highest bliss. It seems to me that the door to Vaikuntha (the divine realm) has definitely opened for you.”

Naciketah said, “O Yamaraja, there is no need to praise me. Kindly instruct me about that factual substance you understand to be distinct from dharma and adharma, distinct from cause and effect, and distinct from past and future.”

Yama continued, “I will briefly describe the form of that brahma, whose glories are sung by the Vedas, for whose satisfaction people perform penance and fire rituals, and for whose satisfaction the brahmacarls study the Vedas and serve the acaryas. Listen. You should consider 'от as the very form of brahma. The imperishable brahma is renowned in the form of ‘paramaksara', the Supreme Syllable. Those who have realized this brahma, which is the chief and ultimate shelter of all, are being worshipped in the next world. Just as Bhagavan has no birth and death, similarly the soul who knows Bhagavan also has no birth and death. Surrendering to the sabda-brahma or nama-brahma, which is non-different from Bhagavan, is the only way for living beings to attain auspiciousness.

*nayam atma pravacanena labhyo na*  
*medhaya na bahudha srutena*  
*yam evaisa vrnute tena labhyas tasyaisa*  
*atma vivrnute tanum svam*

*Katha Upanisad 1.2.23*

**Bhagavan cannot be known through intellect or erudition. One cannot attain Him even after much hearing. But Bhagavan, the self-illuminated Supreme Soul, reveals His own spiritual body to that exclusively surrendered soul whom He personally accepts. This is the attainment of the supreme abode of Visnu.**

## Chapter 4 King janasruti and raikva

There was once a king named Janasruti. He had established many kitchens with the intention that everyone should receive the food offered by him. He became famous as the epitome of charity and generosity.

Some holy sages among the demigods, being very pleased by the good qualities of this charitable king, decided to help him. So one night during the summer season, they assumed the forms of swans and began to fly around in the sky. As it was a summer night, the king was sleeping on the roof of the palace. The sages disguised as swans were flying in the sky exactly above the king. Among those swans, the one who was flying behind told the swan flying in front of him, “Don’t you know that King Janasruti’s splendour has extended up to the sky? So don’t pass through it, otherwise you will get burnt by his effulgence.”

The swan in front responded, “Pray tell, who is this individual whom you praise with such magnificence? Is he Sakatavana (the cart-dweller) Raikva?”

The other swan inquired, “Who is Sakatavana Raikva?”

The swan in front explained, "Whatever subject there is to be known in this world is all perfectly known to Raikva. This is a brief introduction to Raikva’s identity.”

King Janasruti was overhearing the whole conversation. He immediately got up from his bed and approached his charioteer. “Who is this cart-dwelling Raikva? Find out his whereabouts. After hearing about Raikva from the swans, I have an ardent desire to meet him.”

The charioteer searched for Raikva everywhere. Despite his arduous investigation, he was unable to find him. Then the king advised his charioteer to search in the secluded places where the hermit sages live. Following the order of the king, the charioteer again set out to search for Raikva. At long last, in a secluded place, he came across a man sitting beneath a cart scratching his itching body. The charioteer asked that person, “Are you Sakatavana Raikva?”

That sage confirmed, “Yes.” Then the charioteer quickly returned to inform the king that he had finally discovered Sakatavana Raikva.

Then King Janasruti gathered six hundred cows, a golden necklace and a chariot to offer as gifts, and came before the sage Raikva. Offering all these gifts, the king asked him, “Which God do you worship? Kindly teach me something about that God.”

Raikva told the king, “O sudra (one belonging to the labourer class)! You are now afflicted by lamentation. Therefore, you are not a ksatriya (one belonging to the royal administrative class), you are a sudra. You can keep all these elephants, the golden necklace and the chariot.”

After thoughtful consideration, the king again approached Raikva with one thousand cows, a golden necklace, a chariot and his own daughter. “Please accept these gifts. Kindly accept my daughter in marriage and also accept this village as your asrama and make my life successful. Now kindly instruct me about your God.”

Raikva again told the king, “O grief-stricken sudra! Without rendering any service to Sri Guru, have you come to gain knowledge just by offering him daksina (gifts)?”

After speaking in this way, Raikva instructed King Janasruti on the subject of prana-vidya, the root of knowledge.

Through the narration of this episode, the Srutis (Vedas) teach that the son of a brahmana is not necessarily a brahmana; the son of a ksatriya is not necessarily a ksatriya; the son of a vaisya is not necessarily a vaisya; and the son of a sudra is not necessarily a sudra. A person’s caste is determined by his qualities alone. This is the scientific social ethic of the scriptures. Despite having a vast kingdom and being a renowned warrior; despite appearing before the sage with so many cows, a golden necklace, a chariot, the princess and the village as offerings, the sage Raikva received King Janasruti by addressing him as a ‘sudra’ because Raikva knew that the king was grief-stricken. King Janasruti understood that, compared to the sage Raikva, his greatness was nothing. Then grief arose in his heart. Raikva observed that, despite the king being briefly absorbed in grief, the inclination to serve Sri Guru was present in his heart. Therefore, he instructed King Janasruti in the teachings of тйlа-jnana, the root of knowledge (prana-vidva).

Another lesson learned from this parable is that one cannot assess a bona fide guru's greatness (gurutva) solely on the basis of his external appearance or behaviour. To consider Sri Gurudeva to be an ordinary human being is an offence. Despite seeing Raikva sitting beneath a cart scratching his itching body, King Janasruti never saw his gurudeva, the knower of the Supreme Reality (parabrahmavid), with material vision (prakrta-buddhi). King Janasruti did not project an imaginary conception onto Raikva Muni, based upon knowledge acquired through the senses (pratyaksa-jnana), by mistakenly thinking that Raikva Muni was entangled in the pleasures and pains of the body like the materially conditioned jivas. If he had done so, he would have been deprived of the great fortune of acquiring this confidential knowledge.

Approximately twenty-five to thirty years ago, the crown jewel of the dynasty of saints beyond scriptural obligations (avadhutas), Srila Gaura Kisora dasa Gosvami Prabhu, manifested his divine play by residing in the restroom of a pilgrims’ hostel in Navadvipa. Srila Sanatana Gosvami Prabhu manifested the pastime of contracting a severe skin infection in his sacred body. One will be deprived of the essence of these pastimes by trying to comprehend them through physical sense perception (pratyaksa-jnana) and maintaining material conceptions (prakrta-buddhi). Janasruti did not harbour the notion that his guru, Raikva Muni, was an ordinary mortal being (manava-buddhi). When Janasruti completely surrendered, and especially, when he did not become agitated by the harsh words of Sri Gurudeva, the sage Raikva considered him to be a worthy recipient and instructed him in transcendental wisdom.

Raikva Muni instructed Janasruti that only offering accumulated worldly items as daksina (sacrificial remuneration) is not service to Sri Guru. One must serve the guru along with the offering of his entire self (sarvatma-samarpana). Without the desire to hear the message (vani) of Sri Guru, brahma-vidya (knowledge of the ultimate reality) cannot be attained.

## Chapter 5 Satyakama jabala

Once, the young son of a widow named Jabala said to his mother, “O Mother, I will become a brahmacari (celibate student) and reside in the guru’s house. Tell me, what is the name of our gotra (genealogical lineage)?”

Jabala replied to her son, “Dear child! I don’t know the name of your gotra. During my youth, I served in many households as a maid and it was during that time that you were born to me. Therefore, I do not know in which gotra you were born. My name is Jabala, and your name is Satyakama. You should just say this to the acarya (spiritual preceptor).”

Satyakama went to the acarya, Rsi Haridrumata Gautama, and told him about his desire to reside in the guru’s residence. When Gautama Rsi asked the boy about his gotra, the boy replied, “Revered one! I do not know my gotra. I asked my mother, but she said that while working in many households, she gave birth to me. My mother’s name is Jabala, and my name is Satyakama.”

Upon hearing the honest and simple words of the boy, the sage Gautama became extremely satisfied. He said, “A non-brahmana can never speak the truth with such simplicity and sincerity. Bring some firewood for the ritual sacrifice. I will perform your sacred thread ceremony (upanayana-samskara), and you should never waver from the path of truth.”

Gautama performed Satyakama’s upanayana-samskara and entrusted him with the responsibility of service. Gautama brought four hundred weak cows from his cattle shed and instructed Satyakama to take them to graze upon the pastures. While taking the cows to the meadow, Satyakama said, “I will tend to these four hundred cows in such a way that they will become a herd a thousand strong, and then I will return.” After some time, by the fruit of Satyakama’s dedicated service, the herd indeed became a thousand strong.

One day, a bull called out to Satyakama and said, “Saumya! О gentle one! We have reached a count of one thousand. Now, please take us back to the guru's asrama'' Vayu, the demigod of the wind, had temporarily entered into this bull and possessed him. He called Satyakama and imparted unto him the knowledge of eka-pada vibhuti (one fourth of the splendour of brahman). He also promised to arrange for Satyakama to be instructed in the second quarter of the splendour of brahman by the demigod of fire, Agni. The next day, as Satyakama was leading the cows towards the asrama, evening fell. After bringing the cows to a place for them to rest, Satyakama lit a fire. Then, the fire-god appeared in that fire and, beckoning Satyakama, Agni made him hear the narration of the second quarter of the splendour of brahman. He also promised that Surya, the demigod of the sun, would assume the form of a swan and instruct him in the third quarter.

The next day, Satyakama set out to herd the cows towards the asrama, but evening fell again during their journey. When Satyakama had secured the cows for the night, Surya, in the form of a swan, imparted the teachings of the third quarter of the splendour of brahman and informed Satyakama that the demigod of the life-force (prana), in the form of an aquatic bird called 'madgu' (diver-bird), would provide Satyakama the instruction for the fourth pada.

Satyakama was on his way to the asrama the following day when evening fell once more. He lit a fire again. At that moment, the demigod of the life-force (prana), in the form of a diver-bird, narrated the final quarter of the splendour of brahman to Satyakama.

Satyakama, who had now become brahmavit, a knower of the Absolute Truth, returned to the asrama of his guru. When Gautama Rsi saw him, he could understand that Satyakama had attained divine knowledge as the fruit of his guileless service (niskapata-seva) and hearing (sravana). Acarya Gautama asked Satyakama, “Who has imparted to you the knowledge of the Supreme brahman?”

Satyakama replied, “I have not been instructed by a human being. In addition to their instructions, please instruct me again for the attainment of perfection, because I have heard, ‘Instructions given directly by the acarya cause one to attain perfection.’”

Upon hearing Satyakama’s words, Guru Gautama gave him the same instructions. Satyakama listened with faith and reverence and eventually became an acarya himself. A sage named Upakosala received the teachings of brahma-vidya from Acarya Satyakama.

From the story of Satyakama and Jabala, we learn that the Vedas identify ‘brahmanatva’, the characteristic of being a brahmana, with simplicity (saralata) and the absence of deceitfulness (niskapata). Even though Satyakama was the son of a maidservant, when Acarya Gautama saw his simplicity and sincerity, he granted him the eligibility to attain the status of an acarya and a brahmana. Therefore, being a brahmana is not solely determined by one’s birth in a brahmana caste. Whether a person is born in a brahmana lineage or not, he is called a brahmana based on his conduct, nature and attributes. The best of the maha-bhagavatas, Sri Gurudeva, is a paramarthika-brahmana, a transcendental brahmana who informs us about eternal service.

Satyakama’s ideal of serving his guru is worth emulating by every sincere individual. How enthusiastically and selflessly he nurtured and increased his guru’s herd of cows, without considering his own comfort. While Satyakama came to the guru’s asrama with the aim of realizing brahman, he never harboured misguided notions (durbuddhi) such as, “I will just sit down and meditate.” Instead, he happily made a vow, “I should increase Gurudeva’s herd of cows.” Through his sincere service to the guru and unwavering commitment, he gained knowledge of Bhagavan, the Supreme Personality of Godhead. Being pleased by his sincere and straightforward service to Sri Guru, the demigods personally imparted to him the truths about the Supreme Lord. Yet without becoming proud, he once again listened to the knowledge of the Absolute directly from the mouth of his gurudeva.

In this way, Satyakama has imparted to us the lesson that everyone must hear the glories of the Supreme Being directly from the lotus mouth of a mahanta-guru, a spiritual master who is physically present in this world. Only those who are simple (sarala), sincere (niskapata), devoted to serving their guru (guru-seva parayana) and humble (dina-citta) can bring about auspiciousness for others. Only they can become acaryas and preach their guru’s teachings. It is only through the hearing process (srota-patha) in the master-disciple tradition (guru-sisya-parampara) that the eternal stream of spiritual knowledge (amnaya-dhara) continues to flow in this world.

## Chapter 6 Upamanyu

Ayoda Dhaumya Muni had a disciple named Upamanyu. On the order of his gurudeva, Upamanyu used to take care of his guru’s cows and take them to graze. Gurudeva had instructed Upamanyu to tend to the cows throughout the day and return to the asrama in the evening. Every day, on returning to the asrama in the evening, Upamanyu used to bow down before his guru and feel content that he had fulfilled his duties.

One day, when the acarya noticed that Upamanyu was becoming plump, he said, “My dear Upamanyu, day by day, you are gaining weight. What do you eat?”

Upamanyu replied, “Lord, I depend on alms for my sustenance.”

Upon hearing this, the guru said, “Look, it is not appropriate to accept any food collected as alms without informing me.” Upamanyu, obediently following his guru’s order, offered all his collected alms to the guru. The acarya accepted all the alms but did not give anything . back to Upamanyu.

A few days later, after tending to the cows and returning to the asrama, the acarya asked, “My dear Upamanyu, I accept all the alms you bring, yet you still appear so plump. Do you eat somewhere else?”

Upamanyu replied, “Lord, I collect alms once, offer them to you, and then again I beg a handful of grains as my meal the second time.”

The acarya responded, “Look, this is not appropriate conduct or righteous behavior. By doing this, you prevent others from receiving alms, and gradually you may also become greedy.”

Upamanyu obediently followed his guru’s command. One evening, when Upamanyu returned from herding the cows, the acarya asked, “My dear Upamanyu, the alms you collect are already being accepted by me, and after my prohibition, you do not accept alms for yourself. Nevertheless, you seem to be even fatter than before. What have you been eating lately?”

Upamanyu replied, “I sustain myself by drinking the milk of the

cows.”

The acarya responded, “I never gave you permission to drink the milk of the cows. It is quite improper to drink the cows’ milk.”

Upamanyu begged his gurudeva for forgiveness for his offence. One day after returning from tending the cows, he prostrated himself before his guru. The guru asked, “My dear child, Upamanyu! You neither eat the alms nor request for them a second time. I have also prohibited you from drinking the cows’ milk, yet why are you getting fatter? What are you eating now?”

Upamanyu replied, “I consume the foam produced from the mouths of the young calves when they drink from their mother’s udder.”

The acarya responded, “The peaceful young calves, showing compassion for you, produce foam in greater quantity. Therefore, you are preventing them from taking their share. Please do not repeat this behaviour.”

Taking the order of his guru upon his head, Upamanyu began to serve his guru with renewed diligence. One day, while herding the cows in the forest, he became extremely hungry. To maintain his energy for the sake of his guru's service, he consumed some leaves of the arka plant (Asclepias gigantea). However, as a result of eating the arka leaves, Upamanyu developed a severe eye condition and became blind. Blind and alone, Upamanyu wandered and eventually fell into a well.

Meanwhile, evening was approaching and Upamanyu had not returned from the pastures. Seeing this, the acarya became worried and began to express his concerns to the other disciples. “I had instructed Upamanyu to refrain from all kinds of food. He may be upset with me, and that is why he has not returned.” Thinking in this way, the acarya took some disciples and entered the forest, calling out loudly for Upamanyu. Upon hearing the acarya’s voice, Upamanyu, from inside the well, called out to his gurudeva with great humility.

Upon seeing Upamanyu in this condition, the guru instructed him to recite a prayer from the Rg Veda to the celestial physicians of the demigods, the Asvini Kumaras, because Upamanyu’s vision could be restored by their grace. The Asvini Kumaras, pleased with Upamanyu’s prayers, gave him a cake and instructed him to consume it. “By eating this, your ailment will be cured and you will have good eyesight for the rest of your life.”

Upamanyu, with deep respect for his gurudeva replied, “I cannot accept this food without offering it to my guru and obtaining his permission”

The Asvini Kumaras responded, “Previously, your guru had also chanted our prayers. At that time we gave him this cake and he accepted it without the permission of his gurudeva. Just as your guru accepted the food we provided without receiving an order or permission, you should also do the same.”

Upamanyu, with folded hands and great humility, said to the AsvinI Kumaras, “I humbly request that you do not make this request of me. Without offering this cake to my guru, I cannot accept it.”

Seeing Upamanyu’s unwavering devotion towards his guru, the AsvinI Kumaras became greatly satisfied. They blessed Upamanyu saying, “All your teeth will become golden. Once again you will regain your eyesight and you will attain all auspiciousness.”

After regaining his eyesight, Upamanyu approached his guru, prostrated himself at his holy feet and explained everything that had happened. The acarya was immensely pleased and pronounced a blessing upon Upamanyu, “You have passed the test. May your memory be illumined by all the Vedas and all the dharma scriptures. Your welfare is assured.”

This episode from thtMahabharata (AdiParva 3-34-77) illustrates the ideal of a guru-sevaka. A true servant of guru will never try to enjoy the guru’s possessions. Dedication to service is his eternal duty.

No matter how strict and intense the command of Sri Guru may be, a genuine servant of guru will obey it with an unwavering and happy heart. A true guru-sevaka blissfully accepts his guru’s order, setting aside his own comforts, without concern even for his own life.

A servant of Sri Guru should reflect in this way:

*tomara sevaya, duhkha haya jatd,*  
*seo to’parama sukha*  
*seva-sukha-duhkha, parama-sampada,*  
*nasaye avidya-duhkha*

*Saranagati, Atma-nivedana Song Eight, verse 4 by Srila Bhaktivinoda Thakura*

**Whatever difficulties I face while rendering service, I accept as my greatest happiness. The comfort and discomfort experienced in service are both supremely valuable as they destroy the suffering of ignorance.**

One will be deprived of success if one imitates (anukarana) the behaviour of Sri Gurudeva. Rather, one should follow (anusarana) his general teachings and specific instructions. Upamanyu did not come under the influence of the Asvini Kumaras’ words and imitate Sri Gurudevas conduct. He was not willing to eat without Gurudeva’s consent. Intelligent people follow the teachings (vani) of the great personalities (mahapurusas). By this alone, they attain the fulfilment of all their cherished desires. Those who serve Sri Guru with this type of mentality (citta-vrtti) will pass all the difficult tests on earth. Realization of all the confidential mysteries of the scriptures will become manifest in their hearts and shine with splendour within their memory forever. Only by the grace of Sri Guru is it possible to attain Sri Krsna’s service, the ultimate limit of all auspiciousness.

## Chapter 7 Arjuna and Ekalavya

Ekalavya’s devotion to his guru (described in Mahabharata, Adi Parva 134.2-3) is widely considered an ideal for everyone, but it is necessary to consider a specific perspective on this matter.

King Hiranyadhanus had a son named Ekalavya, who belonged to the Nisada (candala) community. Prince Ekalavya approached Guru Dronacarya to receive training in weaponry (astra-vidya), but Dronacarya declined to teach him as Ekalavya came from a lower caste. Undeterred, Ekalavya made a solemn vow to learn archery from Dronacarya. So he went to the forest and made an idol of Guru Dronacarya from clay. Practising diligently before this idol, which was his guru only in his imagination, Ekalavya gradually achieved mastery in the art of archery.

Arjuna was Dronacarya’s favourite disciple, and Dronacarya once told him that none of his other students would receive more training than him.

One day, on the order of Dronacarya, the Pandavas and Kauravas entered a forest to catch a deer. While they were in the forest, they encountered a strange sight. A hunting dog, who was barking, suddenly became silent. His mouth had been filled by seven arrows at once without causing injury. They were all astonished and thought, “Whoever shot these arrows must be more powerful than the Pandavas.” Thinking in this way, they began to search for that skilled person. After some investigation, they discovered that it was Ekalavya, the son of King Hiranyadhanu, who had shot the arrows.

The Pandavas returned to the capital and narrated this amazing incident to Dronacarya. Arjuna humbly told his teacher, “There is another student of yours who is even more skilled than me in archery.”

Upon hearing this, Dronacarya was surprised. Accompanied by Arjuna, Dronacarya went to the forest and saw how Ekalavya was continuously raining arrows with great absorption. Dronacarya approached Ekalavya. Seeing Dronacarya suddenly appear before him, Ekalavya bowed down and introduced himself as his disciple. Dronacarya told Ekalavya, “Give me the guru-daksina (offering to the teacher).”

Ekalavya responded, “I will give whatever you order me to offer.” Then Dronacarya asked Ekalavya to cut off the thumb of his right hand and offer it as his guru-daksina. Ekalavya immediately followed his guru's instruction without any argument.

Despite being neglected due to his low caste, Ekalavya kept his faith in Guru Dronacarya. He established the ideal of devotion to the guru by making a clay idol of him. The ordinary people of this world consider that Arjuna felt envious of Ekalavya for being a more skilled archer than himself, and therefore, he tried to destroy Ekalavya through his guru. But a devotee never entertains such a false notion. Bhagavan, the Supreme Lord, is the Ultimate Truth (param satyam). The Lord, devotion and the devotee (Bhagavan, bhakti and bhakta) are the three aspects of the one truth (trisatya). Everything about a devotee is good. The non-devotee has no good qualities. The apparent virtues of a nondevotee are actually faults because they are not engaged in satisfying the senses of Bhagavan. They consider worldly morality (prakrta niti) to be greater than God. They are unable to grasp this ultimate truth. Such persons are called impersonalists (nirvisesavadi). They do not accept Bhagavan, bhakti and bhakta.

What was Ekalavya’s fault? This subject is worthy of our deliberation. Did Guru Dronacarya refuse to provide Ekalavya with archery education because he perceived Ekalavya as belonging to a lower caste or was it for some other reason? It was Ekalavya’s duty to follow the guru's order, but he did not do that. Rather, a desire to excel and become great awakened in Ekalavya.

It is true that without a guru's guidance (guru-karana), a task is not accomplished. Therefore, Ekalavya made an idol of Guru Dronacarya. Whether his desire was to become great or to receive education in archery, his primary purpose was to satisfy his own senses (indriya-trpti). It was not his exclusive desire to sacrifice himself for his guru's desire. Some may also argue that, in the end, Ekalavya complied with his guru’s request with a happy heart. However, on profound and subtle reflection, it is seen that Ekalavya considered social ethics (niti) greater than transcendental devotion (aprakrta bhakti). Gurudeva can ask anything as a daksina and it is a disciple’s duty to comply. Following this principle, he offered the guru his thumb as daksina when requested. Ekalavya cut off his thumb to become great from the perspective of social ethics (niti). In reality, Ekalavya did not make the offering out of natural devotion (svabhavika-bhakti). The function of bhakti is natural (svabhavika) and simple (sarala), without an egotistic motive.

If there was any unmotivated (ahaituki) and spontaneous devotion (svabhaviki bhakti) in Ekalavya’s heart towards Bhagavan, Sri Guru and the saints, then Guru Dronacarya, Arjuna, the best of devotees, and Sri Krsna would not have been angered by Ekalavya’s behaviour. Guru Dronacarya did not approve of Ekalavya’s ambition to become great or his desire to learn archery. Ekalavya endeavoured to fulfil his strong aspiration to become even greater than Arjuna, the best of the Lord’s devotees.

The desire to become greater than another devotee is not devotion. It is the tendency of the atibadi apasampradaya (deviant sects) and non-devotees. The endeavour to remain behind the vaisnavas and remain under their guidance is bhakti. However, instead of srota-vidya, that is, learning by hearing directly from the physically present teacher (mahanta-guru), Ekalavya wanted to become great on the strength of his own courage or heroism. This was the point that Arjuna conveyed to his guru. If Arjuna had not mercifully informed his guru about this, then impersonalism (nirvisesavada) would have become victorious. Then the living entities, instead of learning from a physically present teacher (mahanta-guru), would manufacture a lifeless idol of a guru against the guru’s wishes and, in accordance with their own whims, try to gain knowledge or devotion from the idol. This is how atheistic theories (nastika-mata) are established. Therefore, Arjuna has shown only causeless mercy both unto Ekalavya and unto the whole world.

If Ekalavya had been a sincere devotee of his guru, Sri Krsna would never have destroyed such a devoted disciple. Sri Krsna always protects His devotees. However, Ekalavya was killed by Sri Krsna Himself.

Lord Caitanya has said that true devotion cannot be judged merely by external austerities. Demons also perform such austerities that even the demigods cannot perform. In Ekalavya’s case, against his guru’s wishes, he desired to become greater than a vaisnava. Therefore, he was killed by Sri Krsna and attained an impersonal destination (nirvisesa-gati). The demons are killed by Sri Krsna and the devotees are protected by Sri Krsna. The demon Hiranyakasipu and the devotee Prahlada are evidence in this regard. Therefore, we should never try to become ‘greater’ than the vaisnavas by wearing a mask of guru-bhakti. Pure devotees must learn this lesson from the episode of Ekalavya and not become impersonalists. Being more skillful than everyone else in activities is not guru-bhakti. Bhakti entails remaining always under the guidance (anugatya) of the vaisnavas.

## Chapter 8 Duryodhana's illusion (vivarta)

During the burning of the Khandava forest, Arjuna saved a renowned craftsman named Maya Danava. The best of craftsmen, Maya Danava constructed a magnificent royal assembly hall for King Yudhisthira’s Rajasuya-yajna. The sight of this assembly hall, inlaid with jewels, pearls and coral, attracted everyone on earth. On seeing King Yudhisthira’s grandeur in this assembly hall, Duryodhana began to burn in the fire of jealousy.

As an invited guest, Duryodhana first entered the building. Upon seeing the courtyard made of clear crystal in front of the assembly hall, Duryodhana mistook it for a pool. In his confusion, he lifted his cloth slightly higher as if to cross the water. Seeing this, everyone present burst into laughter and clapped their hands. A short distance further, there was a real pool filled with clear water. Duryodhana thought, “I have been embarrassed once by mistakenly perceiving the floor as water. If I repeat the mistake again, there will be no limit to my humiliation. If, on seeing the first courtyard, I have mistaken it for water, then I would certainly also mistake this second courtyard for water.” Thinking in this way, Duryodhana mistook the water for solid ground, fell into the pool and began to sink. Once again, everyone present burst into laughter, clapping their hands in amusement.

After that experience, he mistook a clear crystal wall for an open door and attempted to enter. He bumped his head against the wall and felt concussed. Then he approached what he thought was a massive doorway, but again, he collided with the wall, and his head spun even more. On seeing another huge doorway, he considered that he might face further disgrace, so he decided not to enter at all. Due to being humiliated in various ways, Duryodhana was consumed by the fire of indignation and he returned home in a deeply distressed state. This incident was also one of the causes of the war between the Kauravas and the Pandavas.

The name ‘Duryodhana’ (duh + yodhana) denotes a sense enjoyer (bhogi) who desires to fight on the path of injustice. Duryodhana’s attitude towards Sri Krsna’s dear devotees, the Pandavas, was: “I will not give them even enough land in which to drive the tip of a needle. I alone will enjoy the whole world all for myself.”

On the other hand, the monarch of dharma, Yudhisthira (yudhi + sthira) was steadfast (sthira) in the war (yuddha) for truth and justice. His only purpose was to fulfil Sri Krsna’s desire. People with Duryodhana’s nature harbour enmity towards the devotees, who strive to fulfil the desire of Sri Guru and Sri Krsna and whose purpose is always fulfilled (satya-sankalpa). The eyes of the envious always interpret the behaviour of devotees in an opposite manner and misconstrue their intentions. By misunderstanding the pure devotees, they never really associate with devotees.

The conditioned souls dedicated to sense enjoyment who become hostile to the devotees perceive water (jala) where there is land (sthala) and land where there is water. In other words, they see those who have shelter as without shelter, and those without shelter as having shelter, and thus they drown. The association of pure devotees is the only shelter (asraya). Association with Maya, the material energy, is the state of being shelterless (nirasraya). When one becomes hostile to the pure devotees, he must sink into the deep waters of Maya. Impersonalists (mayavadis) are inimical to the Lord and His devotees. Therefore, they become subject to illusory conceptions (vivarta) and attain a lower destination.

Mayavadis: those who, having accepted illusion (Maya), engage in philosophical disputes (vivada). They profess that parabrahman Bhagavan, who is the controller of Maya, becomes entangled in Maya and appears in the form of a jiva or that Narayana appears disguised as a poverty-stricken person. In reality, Bhagavan is the controller of Maya (mayadhisa), and His separated atomic conscious parts, the individual spirit souls (jivas), are under the influence of Maya.

## Chapter 9 Dhritarastha breaks the iron statue of Bhima

The Pandavas had slain the hundred sons of Dhrtarastra. Among the Pandavas, Bhimasena had killed most of Dhrtarastra’s sons. It was Bhima who broke Duryodhana’s thigh and drank the blood from Duhsasana’s chest. Therefore, Dhrtarastra was furious with Bhima.

After the demise of the Kauravas, when the Pandavas, headed by Yudhisthira, were leaving Hastinapura, they went to pay their respects to the elderly Kuru king. Dhrtarastra, although dissatisfied, embraced Yudhisthira and offered him consolation. Then he started searching for Bhima, intending to kill him on the pretext of an embrace. The Lord, Sri Krsna, who is situated in the heart of everyone as antaryami, could understand the hidden intent of the blind king, so He had made an iron statue of Bhima to thwart his plan. Dhrtarastra’s evil intention blazed within him like fire. At that moment, when Sri Krsna presented the iron statue of Bhima before the deceitful Dhrtarastra, he remembered the killing of his hundred sons. Burning with anger, he embraced the iron statue of Bhima and crushed it to powder in a fit of rage.

Dhrtarastra had the immense strength of many elephants. His embrace was so strong that he could even pulverize solid iron. Nevertheless, his chest was wounded in the process and he began to vomit blood. All the witnesses were astonished to see how Dhrtarastra had hidden his desire and his intense spirit of vengeance.

‘Dhrtarastra’ literally means ‘one by whom a kingdom (rajya) or nation (rastra) is held (dhrta)’. That is, one who considers the knowledge and consciousness of this kingdom of Maya (the inert material universe) to be the only worthy shelter is ‘Dhrtarastra’. A person whose heart is seized by worldly worries and who has no relation with the non-material substance (aprakrta-vastu) and divine play (cid-vilasa) beyond matter is the very emblem of‘Dhrtarastra’.

Dhrtarastra, being born blind, never saw the variety of the world during his lifetime. In other words, he was an impersonalist (nirvisesavadi). Impersonalists are those who do not acknowledge the divine form and qualities of God. Just like Dhrtarastra, materialists and impersonalists give up the association of the saintly devotees who are beyond the influence of Maya. Being controlled by an unjust spirit of revenge, impersonalists are always ready to denigrate Sri Krsna’s devotees.

Such persons can only crush and destroy material objects to show off their strength. Ultimately, they fall into the jaws of death, i.e. they attain impersonal destinations (nirvisesa gati). Sri Krsna always protects His surrendered devotees. The impersonalists cannot touch even one hair of the devotees. Like Dhrtarastra, they exhaust their own strength and end up vomiting blood in vain.

## Chapter 10 Indra the pig and lord Brahma

Indra is the king of heavenly demigods and Brhaspati is his guru. Once, Brhaspati came to give advice to Indra, but instead of welcoming, honouring, and worshipping his guru, Indra was intoxicated from revelling with the celestial nymphs (apsaras). Therefore, Brhaspati cursed Indra to descend to earth in the form of a pig. Now, Indra the pig found pleasure in revelling with a female pig and enjoyed eating stool. Later, his partner gave birth to many piglets.

One day, while wandering, Lord Brahma came to Indra the pig and witnessing Indra’s pitiful condition, felt deeply saddened. Speaking to Indra with a heavy heart, he said, “Hey Indra, you are entitled to the throne of heaven. Instead of partaking in the ambrosial feast of Amaravati, the celestial realm, why are you indulging in a meal of stools here?”

Upon hearing Lord Brahma’s words, the pig Indra, overwhelmed with anger, charged at Brahma, along with his family, and attempted to bite him. However, Lord Brahma persisted in his effort to benefit Indra. Brahma tried to explain the true nature of Indra in every way possible. The more Brahma tried to help Indra, the more Indra considered Brahma as his enemy. Indra the pig thought eating stool, roaming freely in the mud, and indulging in carnal pleasures to be the only happiness for him and his family. He considered his piglets to be his own intimate family and was not willing to give up these objects of attachment. But compassionate Brahma, who feels the distress of others, considered, “This pig’s attachment to his body and his family is the root of all impediments. As long as he remains attached to material enjoyment, he will not accept any good advice. I must benefit Indra one way or another.”

Thinking in this way, Brahma began to kill the children of Indra the pig, one by one. Seeing his children being killed right in front of his eyes, the pig Indra burned with rage and charged at Brahma to bite him to death. Brahma began to instruct him on the transient nature of the material world, but his words did not enter Indra’s ears. Then Brahma also killed the sow, Indra’s partner. Now all four directions seemed filled with darkness for Indra. All his dear ones were dead. Understanding this, Indra said to Brahma, “Sir, you have killed all my dear ones. Before all this, you were asking me to go to heaven. All right, I ask you, is there such delicious stool in heaven as there is here? Is there such a peaceful place there? Are female pigs available there?”

Brahma replied, “You are the official ruler of heaven. You have only come here because you were cursed as a result of your own actions. When you are established in that form, you will not find this stool delicious. At that time, you will dine on nectar and you will not desire to enjoy the disgusting sense gratification of sexual union with pigs. Rather, you will be able to achieve the greatest happiness.”

After losing his wife and children, the pig Indra realized the impermanence of this world. Now, he began to hear Brahma’s teachings with faith and reverence. He inquired about how to return to his own kingdom in heaven and Brahma instructed him accordingly. By faithfully following these instructions, Indra eventually left the life of a pig and again attained the status of Indra, the monarch of heaven.

The conditioned living entities, bound by Maya in this material world, are also in this same situation. By intrinsic nature, the soul is a servant of Sri Krsna. Serving Sri Krsna is the soul’s eternal function (nitya-dharma). The spiritual world of Goloka is his true abode. The purpose of his existence is the joy of serving Sri Krsna. However, impelled by misfortune, when the soul is deprived of the joy of service, he wanders lifetime after lifetime in various species. Then, he forgets his intrinsic function (svarupa-dharma) and eternal purpose (nitya-prayojana). Being attached to the physical body, the soul considers the body to be his true self and remains in a state of bodily identification (dehatma-buddhi). The soul begins to perceive other bodies associated with his body as intimate relatives (svajana). He begins to consider the comfort and pleasure of the body as his ultimate purpose. When the soul forgets his true nature, then the supremely compassionate Vaisnava Thakura, the devotee of Sri Krsna, inspired by Sri Krsna, manifests in the form of Sri Guru before the soul. Sri Guru instructs the soul about his eternal nature. However, the conditioned souls are extremely attached to the abominable stool of material sense objects. Due to this, as much as the supremely compassionate guru and vaisnavas give spiritual instruction, the soul starts to perceive them as his enemy and tries to attack them in various ways.

Still, Sri Guru and the vaisnavas feel distressed over the plight of the conditioned souls, who are engrossed in tasting the stool of material sense objects, and show unlimited compassion for the sake of bringing about their spiritual welfare. Durga Devi, the superintendent of this fort of the material world, deprives the conditioned souls of the sense objects, to which they are attached, and throws them into the suffering of worldly calamity for the sake of purification. In this way, the soul realizes the temporary nature of this world. Only then may the auspicious teachings of Sri Guru and the vaisnavas enter the soul’s ears. The soul then associates with the saints (sadhu-sanga) with faith (sraddha) and takes shelter of the lotus feet of the guru (guru-pada-asraya). As he goes on serving Bhagavan, he gradually attains his eternal form (nitya-svarupa). Step by step, achieving svarupa-siddhi and then vastu-siddhi, that servant goes to Goloka Vrndavana and enters into the eternal bliss of serving the lotus feet of Sri Krsna.

Svarupa-siddhi: Only a jiva with pure consciousness is a servant of Sri Krsna (krsna-dasa). This is everyone’s true identity; this is their intrinsic nature (svarupa). The body, mind, words and all the senses remain engaged at all times in the service of Bhagavan concomitant with the attainment of this svarupa. Only this state of being is called svarupa-siddhi or jivan-mukta-dasa, the state of being liberated while still alive in this world. Svarupa-siddhi arises from total absorption (abhinivesa) in service. Only the devotees who have attained svarupa-siddhi are naturally swan-like saints (sahaja paramahamsa).

Vastu-siddhi (the attainment of krsna-prema): The devotees who have attained vastu-siddhi are factually manifest, by Sri Krsna’s mercy, in a spiritual body (siddha-deha) in the form of His associate in His Vraja pastimes. This is vastu-siddhi and the ultimate fruit of devotion.

## Chapter 11 Ravana’s abduction of illusory Sita

Everyone knows that the king of Lanka, Ravana, abducted Lord Ramacandra’s Goddess of Fortune, Sita, the daughter of Janaka Maharaja, and kept Her captive in the Asoka Groves for many days. Lord Ramacandra, after constructing a bridge across the ocean, arrived in Lanka, defeated Ravana and rescued Sita Devi. To prove the purity of Her character, Lord Rama asked SIta to undergo the test of fire (agni-pariksa). When Sita Devi emerged unscathed from the trial of fire, Lord Ramacandra again accepted Her as His wife.

Sri Ramacandra is directly Lord Visnu, and Sita Devi is His eternal consort, the Goddess of Fortune (Svayam-Laksmi). Sri Ramacandra did not accept anyone else as His wife, and SIta Devi also knew nothing but Lord Rama. Sri Rama and Sita Devi are not a human male and female couple or ordinary souls (jivas). Ravana was a demonic jiva. The demons do not have the capacity to touch or abduct the consort of the Lord. What to speak of touching, they cannot even see Her with their eyes. The Vedas, Puranas, and other scriptures assert that mortal beings cannot perceive immortal entities. As such, we souls in the state of material bondage (baddha-jivas) cannot behold God with our fleshly eyes (carma-caksu).

When Sri Caitanyadeva was travelling in South India, He visited the house of a Rama devotee in Madurai to accept alms. This brahmana had already invited Sri Caitanyadeva beforehand, but when He arrived at the brahmana’s house after bathing in the Krtamala River in the afternoon, He noticed that the brahmana had made no arrangements for cooking. When Mahaprabhu inquired about the reason, the brahmana said, “Sita Devi will make the cooking arrangements once Laksmana returns from the forest with vegetables and fruits.” Then the brahmana slowly began to prepare the kitchen. In the late afternoon, Mahaprabhu accepted alms at the brahmana’s house, but the brahmana did not eat anything. He continued to fast completely. Seeing the brahmana’s distress, Mahaprabhu inquired about the reason. The brahmana replied, “I do not wish to stay alive any longer. I will enter a fire or drown myself. Sita Devi, the mother of the universe, has been touched by the demon Ravana. Hearing this, I have no desire to live anymore. My heart is burning in distress.”

Mahaprabhu explained to the brahmana, “Sita Devi is directly Laksmi Herself, the Goddess of Fortune. Her form is sac-cid-ananda, the embodiment of transcendental existence, consciousness and bliss. What to speak of touching Her, the demon could never even see Her. Ravana kidnapped an illusory Sita (Maya Sita). When Ravana arrived, Sita Devi disappeared and sent a Maya Sita before Ravana.”

When Sri Caitanyadeva visited Setubandha Ramesvara, He observed that the brahmanas there were reciting passages from the Кurmа Purana. He heard them recite the story of how Sita Devi, the crest-jewel of chaste ladies, upon seeing Ravana, accepted the shelter of Agnideva, the fire-god. Agnideva then presented an illusory copy of Sita (chaya Sita) and the original Sita Devi remained in Agnipuri, the abode of the fire-god. It was this illusory Sita that Ravana abducted, mistaking her for the real Sita Devi. Agnideva left the real Sita Devi with Parvati and drove Ravana away by giving him the illusory Sita. Later, when Lord Rama conducted the trial of Sita, it was the illusory Sita who entered the fire. Then Agnideva brought the original Sita Devi and presented Her before Lord Ramacandra. Sri Caitanyadeva obtained this ancient manuscript of the Кurmа Purana from those brahmanas and showed the relevant passage to the Rama bhakta.

This episode contains a greatly significant lesson. Many ignorant people believe that atheists can commit atrocities against devotees, deities of God, the Ganges River and Tulasi Devi. They are convinced that heathens have destroyed deities and vandalized the domes of the Lord’s temples. Some people are such empiricists (pratyaksa-vadis) that they think, “When thieves, gangsters and atheists destroy deities and steal their ornaments, at that time, God has no capacity to stop them.
